Χρήζω πραΰτητος ἐν ᾗ καταλύεται ὁ ἄρχων τοῦ αἰῶνος τούτου διάβολος. Ψυχὴ (82) ἡ ἅπαξ προσδεθεῖσα τῷ πόθῳ τοῦ κτίσαντος, καὶ τοῖς ἐκεῖσε κάλλεσιν ἐνειθισμένη φαιδρύνεσθαι, τὸ περιχαρὲς αὐτῆς καὶ εὔθυμον ὑπὸ τῆς ποικίλης μεταπτώσεως τῶν σαρκικῶν παθημάτων οὐ μεταβάλλει, ἀλλὰ τὸ τοῖς ἄλλοις λυπηρὰ προσθήκην εὐφροσύνης ποιήσεται. Τοῦ Χρυσοστόμου. — Ψυχροῦ πλεονάζοντος παρέσεις καὶ τρόμοι, καὶ ἀποπληξίαι, καὶ μυρία ἕτερα νοσήματα τίκτεται. 99 Ψυχὴ, ὑπὸ ἀθυμίας βληθεῖσα, πρὸς πᾶσαν ἀκρόασιν ἀνεπιτήδειος. Τοῦ θεολόγου. — Ψυχαῖς βεβήλοις οὐδὲν τῶν καλῶν ἀξιόπιστον. Σολομῶντος. — Ψυχὴ (83) ἐν πλησμονῇ οὖσα κηρίοις ἐμπαίζει, ψυχὴ δὲ ἐνδεεῖ καὶ τὰ πικρὰ γλυκέα φαίνεται.
Nili. — Gratias age Deo si in virtute progrediaris; auxilium enim divinum te ad illam protexit.
Bonus vir misericors et commodans dispone sermones suos in judicio, quoniam in æternum non commovebitur. Aristo... Qui mensæ gratia studet amicum habere, Citius eum expellit, illa vacante. Gaudere omnino hominem non licet ; Dolores enim et procellæ subito in eum incidunt Pulvis ex pulvere omnia sunt et in pulverem rur- [sus vadunt, Immisercordia autem viscera plerisque insunt. Demoxeni. — Gratia et benevolentia suadentium sunt, non cogentium. Cyrilli. — Res est vere perniciosa pravis conjungi; certum enim quod scriptum est: Qui tetigerit picem inquinabitur. Oportet majus æmulantes melius accipere; et omnia commoventes bonum est ut concertare ament cum his qui ibi fulgent. Sine intelligentia scienti nihil in operibus bonum est; scientia enim prudentiæ fructus est, imprudentia autem non bonum est. Sirach. — Labia multiloquorum non sua ipsorum enarrabunt. Fenum et virga et onera asino ; panis autem, correctio et opus servo. Joannis. — Hiemis humiditas sanguini immista corpus labefactat. Mansuetudine mihi opus est per quam destruitur princeps hujus sæculi diabolus. Mens semel devincta amore Creatoris, et cœlestibus pulchritudinibus assueta micare, suam lætitiam et serenitatem per varias vicissitudines carnalium passionum non amittit ; sed ex his quæ aliis molesta sunt, gaudii incrementum facit.
Chrysostomi. — Frigido superabundante languores, tremores, apoplexiæ et sexcenti alii morbi nascuntur. Mens postrata ad omnem exhortationem audiendam impropria est. Theologi. — Mentibus profanis nulla bonarum rerum fide digna est. Salomonis. — Anima in saturitate favos respuit, animæ autem esurienti etiam amara dulcia videntur.


[1155] {#pg.117.1155}
Animæ iniquorum immaturæ pereunt. Susurro et bilingnis maledictione dignus; multos enim disperdet pacificos. Anima tumultuosa diaboli sedes. Anima benedicta tota simplex; vir autem iracundus non decorus. Anima mansueta simplicitatis sedes; mens autem iracunda malitiæ artifex. Anima mansueta continebit verba sapientiæ; ducet enim Dominus mansuetos in judicio, magis autem in discretione. Anima recta humilitatis contubernalis; prava autem superbiæ filia. Animæ mansuetorum replebuntur scientia, iracunda autem mens tenebrarum et ignorantiæ socia. Anima, per puritatem Deo unita, ratione alia ad discendum non indigebit, propriam rationem ad initiandum et illuminandum in seipsa habens. Animæ perditio est; malevoli enim opus non rectum. Vituperans est cor malevoli; Nam amare nihil novit nisi dolos moliri. Animæ ægrotantis verba medici sunt. Comedere et libere dabis ingrato, et præterea acerba audies; duplex enim malum inveniet contra te pro beneficiis quæ accepit. Joannis. — Vituperare quemlibet occurrentem non secundum rationem est; vituperare autem virum justum, est evidenter in Deum peccare. Vituperari sapientem a pravis semper vides; malus enim nescit virtutem laudare. Joannis monachi. Micas a datore si esuriens pauper exceperit, Deo illius laudes affert. Sexti. — Mens thesaurus est, boni quidem bonus, mali autem malus. Mens omnis quæ admoneri non sustinet, sanari non potest. Increpatio in ore pravi beneficium reputatur; curam autem semper mendacium et calumniam præstat. Democriti. — Animæ divitiarum fons corporis abstinentia; hoc autem ditescente illa pauper fit.

Σιράχ. — Ψυχὴ (99) θερμὴ, ὡς πῦρ καιόμενον, οὐ σβεσθήσεται, ἕως ἂν καταποθῇ· οὕτω καὶ ἄνθρωπος πόρνος ἐν σώματι σαρκὸς αὐτοῦ. Διαδόχου. — Ψυχὴ, μὴ τῶν κοσμικῶν ἀπαλλαγεῖσα φροντίδων, οὔτε τὸν θεὸν ἀγαπήσει γνησίως, οὔτε διάβολον βδελύξεται ἀξίως· κάλυμμα γὰρ ἅπαξ ἔχει φορτικὸν τὴν μέριμναν τοῦ βίου. Ψυχαγωγοῦσι τοὺς ἀτυχήσαντας οἱ μᾶλλον κακοπραγήσαντες. Σολομῶντος. — Ψέγειν τὸν ἀνεπίληπτον εἰς θεὸν ἁμαρτία (1). Ὥσπερ (2) φύλλον θάλλον ἐπὶ δένδρου δασέος, τὰ μὲν καταβάλλει, ἄλλα δὲ φύει· οὕτω γενεὰ σαρκὸς καὶ αἵματος, ἡ μὲν τελευτᾷ, ἄλλη δὲ γεννᾶται· καὶ πᾶν ἔργον σηπόμενον ἐκλείψει, καὶ ὁ ἐργαζόμενος αὐτὸ 102 μετ᾿ αὐτοῦ ἀπελεύσεται· καὶ οἱ ἄνθρωποι πάντες γῆ καὶ σποδός. Ὥσπερ (3) φωνὴ τῶν ἀκανθῶν ὑπὸ τὸν λέβητα, οὕτω γέλως τῶν ἀφρόνων. Ὥσπερ (4) δοκιμάζεται ἐν καμίνῳ ἄργυρος καὶ χρυσός, οὕτω ἐκλεκταὶ καρδίαι παρὰ θεῷ. Ὥσπερ (5) σπαρτίον κόκκινον χείλη σου. Ὥσπερ (6) ἐν ξύλῳ σκώληξ, οὕτως ἄνδρα ἀπόλλυσι γυνὴ κακοποιός. Ὥσπερ (7) μῆλον ἐν τοῖς ξύλοις τοῦ δρυμοῦ, οὕτως ἀδελφιδός μου ἀνάμεσον τῶν υἱῶν. Ὥσπερ (8) ῥοπὴ ἐκ πλαστίγγων, ὅλος ὁ κόσμος ἐναντίον σου· καὶ ὡς ῥανὶς δρόσου κατελθοῦσα ὀρθρινὴ ἐπὶ γῆς· ἐλεεῖς δὲ πάντας, ὅτι πάντα δύνασαι, καὶ παρορᾷς ἁμαρτήματα ἀνθρώπων εἰς μετάνοιαν.
Ὥσπερ (9) πόλις τὰ τείχη καταβεβλημένη, οὕτως ἀνὴρ ὃς οὐ μετὰ βουλῆς πράσσει. Ὥσπερ (10) ἡ σκιὰ τῷ σώματι, οὕτω ταῖς ψυχαῖς αἱ ἁμαρτίαι παρέπονται, ἐναργεῖς τὰς πράξεις ἐξεικονίζουσαι. Ὥσπερ (11) ὕδωρ πολέμιον πυρὶ, οὕτως ἀμετρία οἴνου λογισμὸν κατασβέννυσι σώφρονα. 103 Ὥσπερ (12) ἕπεται τῷ ἀγαθῷ ἡ ἀφθονία, οὕτως ἀκολουθεῖ τῷ κακῷ ἡ βασκανία. Ὥσπερ (13) ἰὸς σίδηρον, οὕτως φθόνος τὴν ἔχουσαν αὐτὸν ἐξαναλίσκει ψυχήν.
Sirach. — Anima calida, quasi ignis ardens, non exstinguetur donec absorpta fuerit; ita homo quoque impudicus in corpore carnis suæ. Diadochi. — Anima, quæ a mundanis non exsulat curis, neque Deum amabit perfecte, neque a diabolo ut decet abhorrebit: indumentum enim illi molestum est vitæ sollicitudo. Detestanda est res mulier; captos enim semel in suis retibus seducere novit, adjutricem habens voluptatem quæ meditationem parvi facit. Infortunatos ad se trahunt qui plurimum illis nocent. Salomonis. — Irreprehensibilem vituperare est in Deum peccare. Sicut folia crescentia vides in arbore opaca: alia dejiciuntur, alia nascuntur; ita generatio carnis et sanguinis, alia quidem moritur, alia autem gignitur; et omne opus putrefactum deficiet, et qui illud operatur abibit cum eo; universi sane homines terra et cinis. Sicut sonitus spinarum sub olla, ita risus stultorum. Sicut probatur in camino argentum et aurum, ita electa corda a Deo probantur. Sicut vita coccinea labia tua. Sicut in ligno vermis, ita virum perdit mulier maligna. Sicut malus inter ligna sylvæ, ita frater meus dilectus in medio filiorum. Tanquam momentum stateræ, orbis universus ante se; et tanquam gutta roris in aurora super terram cadens, misereris omnium, quia omnia potes et dissimulas peccata hominum ad pœnitentiam.
Sicut urbs muros erutos habens, ita vir qui non agit cum consilio. Sicut umbra corpori, ita animabus peccata adhærent, clare actiones adumbrantia.
Sicut aqua igni inimica, ita immodicum vinum rationem sapientem exstinguit. Sicut bonus invidiam nescit, ita malum invidentia comitatur. Sicut rubigo ferrum, ita invidia mentem quam possidet corrodit.


[1159] {#pg.117.1159}
Sicut ærugo proprius est frumenti morbus, ita  invidia amicitiæ languor. Sicut vermes mollioribus in lignis giguntur, ita dolores mollioribus hominum moribus innascuntur. Sicut igni spontaneum est lucere, et unguento bene olere, ita necessario bonas actiones comitatur utilitas. Sicut gulæ deditum esse molestum est, ita per diuturnam pravitatem corpus dissolvere revera dementia est. Cui non assidet pudor vel metus, os sine freno, et lingua petulans solvitur. Sicut perfectissimorum bonorum moderatio causa est, ita maximorum malorum intemperantia utilissimam æqualitatis legem solvens.  Sicut stultis assuetum est prosperitate extolli, ita effeminatis adversitate dejici; velox enim in utrisque mutatio. Extra animam juventutem vigentem non corrigitur pravitas. Sicut illi qui longam viam perfecerunt iter adhuc facientibus expeditiores et remissiores; ita qui supernum diversorium attigerunt his qui adhuc asperam et abruptam hujus vitæ viam moliuntur feliciores et quietiores sunt. Aures, oculos et linguam diligenter custodi; in omnes irruere nititur peccatum. Senectuti grati puerorum lusus. De nullo tantum gaudet Deus, quantum le emendatione et pœnitentia hominis. Sicut benevolentia aromata suavi odore suo  circumfusum aera replent, ita vir bonus illi ad quem accedere satagit utilitatem præstat.
Platonis. — Sicut in carcere, in hacce vita sumus et custodimur. Et non vides insanum utilia agere, ita non videbis sapientem facere frivola. Ut inter omnia alimenta panis est maxime necessarius, ita inter omnia exercitia mortis meditatio.


[1160] {#pg.117.1160}
"Ωσπερ (14) ἡ ἐρυσίβη ἴδιόν ἐστι τοῦ σίτου νόσημα, οὕως ὁ φθόνος φιλίας ἐστὶν ἀρρώστημα. "Ωσπερ (15) οἱ σκώληκες τοῖς ἁπαλωτέροις τῶν ξύλων ἐντίκτονται, οὕτως αἱ λῦπαι τοῖς μαλακωτέροις ἤθεσι τῶν ἀνθρώπων ἐμφύονται. "Ωσπερ (16) τῷ πυρὶ αὐτομάτως ἕπεται τὸ φωτίζειν καὶ τῷ μύρῳ τὸ εὐωδεῖν, οὕτω καὶ ταῖς ἀγαθαῖς πράξεσιν ἀναγκαίως ἀκολουθεῖ τὸ ὠφέλιμον. "Ωσπερ τὸ εἰς γαστριμαργίαν ἐκκλῖναι· ἐστι χαλεπόν, 104 οὕτω τὸ δι᾿ ἐπιτεταμένης κακουχίας καταβαλεῖν τὸ σῶμα ὡς ἀληθῶς ἀλογώτατον. "Ω (17) μὴ ἐφεδρεύει αἰδώς, ἢ φόβος, ἀχάλινον τὸ στόμα, καὶ ἀνειμένη γλῶσσα λέλυται. "Ωσπερ τῶν τελειοτάτων ἀγαθῶν τὸ συμμετρον αἴτιον, οὕτω τῶν μεγίστων κακῶν ἀμετρία, τὸ ὠφελιμώτατον λύουσα θεσμὸν ἰσότητος. "Ωσπερ ἀπειροκάλων θελητὸν αἵρεσθαι ταῖς εὐπραγίαις, οὕτως ἀνάνδρων τὸ καταπτύσσειν ἐν δυσπραγίαις· ὀξεῖα γὰρ ἐν ἀμφοτέροις ἡ μεταβολή. Ωμῆς (18) δίχα ψυχῆς οὐ καθαίρεται κακία.

Ὦτα καὶ ὀφθαλμοὺς καὶ γλῶσσαν ἀσφαλῶς τήρει· πᾶσι γὰρ ἐπίτροχος ἐστιν ἁμαρτία. Ὡραῖον γήρᾳ παίδων ἀθύρματα. Ὡς (20) οὐδενὶ τοσοῦτον χαίρει Θεός, ὅσον ἀνθρώπου διορθώσει καὶ μετανοίᾳ. "Ωσπερ τὰ εὐώδη τῶν ἀρωμάτων τῆς ἰδίας εὐποιίας τὸν παρακείμενον ἀέρα πλήρη ποιεῖ, οὕτως ἀνὴρ ἀγαθός, πρὸς ὃν εἰσιέναι ἀξιοῖ, παρέχει ὠφέλειαν. Ὡς (22) πασῶν τροφῶν ὁ ἄρτος ἀναγκαιότερος, οὕτως πασῶν ἐργασιῶν ἡ τοῦ θανάτου ἔννοια.

"Ωσπερ (23) οὐ τίκτει πῦρ χιόνα, οὕτως οὐδὲ ὁ τὴν ἐνταῦθα ζητῶν τιμὴν τῆς ἐκεῖ ἀπολαύσει. Ὡς ἄνθος ἀγροῦ πᾶσα γήϊνος φύσις. Θεῖν τὸ φαῦλον καὶ τὰ βελτίω θέλειν Εὐέλπιδος πέφυκεν ἀνθρώπου τόδε. Ὧν οὐδὲν αἱρεῖς (24) τελευτῶν ἐκ τοῦ βίου, τούτων καταφρονεῖν δὲ δέον, ὡς φθειρομένων. Ὠμὸς καὶ ἀπηνὴς ἄνθρωπος οὐ κατευοδοῦται ἐν ὁδῷ. Ὠφελείας στερεῖται τις μὴ παραβάλλων σπουδαίοις ἀνδράσιν. Ὡραϊσμός ἐστι μόνον καὶ μνήμη φέρειν ἄνδρα σπουδαῖον. 106 Ὧν προβαίνει πρὸς ἔργον ὁ λόγος, στάζει χάριν τούτων τὰ χείλη· ὧν δὲ πρὸς ἔργον οὐχ ὁρῶσιν οἱ λόγοι, κενὴ τούτων ἡ γνώμη. Ὡραῖον εὐποιΐα πταισμάτων καθαίρουσα. Ὡς ὄναρ καὶ παίγνιον, ὡς πρὸς τὴν ἄνω ζωὴν ἡ παροῦσα πέφυκεν εὐημερία· πένης γὰρ καὶ πλούσιος ἄμφω κόνις ἐν τάφῳ. Ὡς οἶκ καπνός, ἡ σκιᾶς θεωρία, Δόξα, κράτος, καὶ πλοῦτος ἀνθρώπων ἔνι· Τὰ γὰρ χρόνῳ δοθέντα καὶ λύει χρόνος· Ὥσπερ θανεῖν ὥρισται πᾶσιν ἀνθρώποις, Οὕτως ἄνευ θλίψεως οὐκ ἔστιν ὅλως Περᾶσαι τόνδε τὸν πολύστροφον βίον. Ὥσπερ μελίττης κέντρον ἐμποιεῖ πόνον, Οὕτως μοχθηρός, ὅταν προσβάλλῃ τινί. Ὡραῖον κτῆμα ταπείνωσις· ἡ ταύτης ὢν μέτοχος πολλοὺς τοὺς φίλους ἔχει· πόρρω δὲ ταύτης ὢν τις φίλους ἔχειν οὐ θέλει· μᾶλλον γάρ ἐστιν οὗτος ὑπεροψίας φίλος. Ὡς φεύγειν ἰὸν ὄφεως καλόν, οὕτως ἐντεύξεις τῶν μοχθηρῶν ὡς λύμην· Ὡς ὡραῖον μὴ φεύγειν φίλον ἐντεύξεις, Οὕτως ὡραῖον φεύγειν ἄνδρας ἀτόπους. Ὧν ὁ βίος κοινός, τούτων καὶ αἱ ἐπικαρπίαι κοιναί. Ὧν (25) τοῦ σώματος ἀπαλλαγεὶς οὐ δεήσῃ, καταφρόνει τούτων. 107 Ὡς (26) τεθνηξόμενος τῶν σῶν ἀγαθῶν ἀπόλαυε· Ὡς δὲ βιωσόμενος, φείδου σῶν κτεάνων. Ὡς ἀπὸ τῆς φιλαυτίας τῆς πάντα μισούσης !
Sicut ignis nivem non parit, ita nunquam terrestrem gloriam quærens cœlesti gloria fruetur. Ut flos agri universa terrestris natura. Quod vilius est pellere, et quod optimum velle Hominem decet bonæ spei. Quorum nihil aufers e vita decedens, hæc despicere oportet, ut corrupta. Asper et crudelis homo non prosperabitur in via, Utilitate privatur qui ad viros bonos non accedit. Ornamentum est vel in memoria tantum virum bonum habere. Quorum sermo ad effectum tendit, horum gratiam stillant labia; quorum autem sermones non effectum intuentur, vacuæ eorum est mens. Pulchra est beneficentia quæ crimen expiat. Ut somnium et ludibrium, ita juxta supernam vitam præsens felicitas exsistit; pauper enim et dives ambo pulvis in sepulcro. Ut quidam vapor, vel umbræ apparentia, Gloria potestas, et divitiæ hominum sunt; Quæ enim dedit tempus, tollit tempus. Sicut mori omnibus hominibus statutum est, Ita sine tribulatione non licet omnino Hanc variabilem vitam finire. Sicut apis aculeus dolorem præbet, Ita pravus cum in aliquem irruerit. Pretiosum bonum est humilitas; qui illam sortitus est, multos amicos habet, longe autem ab illa amicos habere non vult aliquis, nam potius est ipse arrogantiæ amicus. Ut venenum serpentis fugere bonum est, ita pravorum conversationem ut pestem. Ut decorum est non fugere amicorum occursus, Ita decorum fugere viros insanos. Quorum vita communis, horum quoque fructus communes. Quibus corpore exutus non egebis, hæc contemne. Ut moriturus tuis bonis utere. Ut autem victurus possessionibus parce. Absit amor proprius qui omnia odit !
Of the Mother of God and Ever-Virgin Mary, in the year 5515, in the 43rd year of Augustus Caesar, in Bethlehem of Judea, on the 8th day before the Calends of January according to the Romans, that is, December 25th. He was baptized at 30 years of age, in the fifteenth year of Tiberius Caesar, on the day before the Ides of January according to the Romans, that is, January 6th. One must know that the city of Bethlehem brought Him forth according to the flesh, the city of Nazareth reared Him, and the city of Capernaum brought Him to full manhood and held Him for 30 years. The description of His appearance was as follows: The magnitude of His stature was that of a just man, neither exceeding the measure nor falling short in the opposite way, absolutely unencumbered as to His flesh; He had great hair, and all the locks were curly, that is, He had twisted hair; He was unshorn, having endless hair, that is, uncut; He parted His locks in two over the forehead, had a sharp nose, was somewhat tawny, with black pupils of the eyes, long fingers, and a moderate mustache—or lip-beard—not allowing this to grow long, but being venerable in its becoming turn. And after 30 years, having chosen disciples and having preached for three years the divine and holy Gospel, then at last He was voluntarily crucified for us under Pontius Pilate, in the 19th year of Tiberius Caesar, on the 10th day before the Calends of April according to the Romans, that is, March 23rd, on the 6th day of the week. And having risen early on the [10th day before the] Calends of April, that is, March 25th, on the 1st day of the week, He was again assumed to where He had descended, on the 3rd of May, on the 5th day of the week.]
UNCERTAIN YEAR
GEORGIDES MONACHUS
EDITOR'S ADMONITION
Georgides, an author of uncertain age, whom one might perhaps conjecture to have written in the 10th or 11th century, can serve as an example of that genre of writing which flourished in the Middle Ages, both in the East and in the West, in the culling of the writings of the Fathers. Furthermore, because the fragments extracted are inscribed with their author's name, they might perhaps serve at some time to identify those authors whose writings lie anonymous in manuscript codices. Cardinal Angelo Mai produced several fragments of Georgides’ book as a specimen; we are publishing the entire book after. Boissonade.
GNOMOLOGIUM. SENTENCES COLLECTED BY JOHN THE MONK, WHO IS ALSO GEORGIDES.
Beholding the beauty of these things written here,
Conspicuous, wondrous, full of doctrine,
Gladly receive them and use them of your own accord.
For the law teaches that the mind prospers when it is directed by good things,
And nature teaches this before the law;
Whence it is fitting to grasp these things here,
Like honey, as food sweet in a wondrous way.
Saint Basil. — Death is the best gift to man; for it frees him from the evils he was bound to commit.
Envy is to be reckoned as nothing by a good man.
It is impossible for human nature not to have something from which it suffers, small or great.
Idleness is the beginning of vice.
Profit from a useless mind is a fault.
The depraved judgment of the mob has conferred power upon the worst.
Virtue preserves the principle of health, but vice is a disease of the mind. Health, indeed, is a good composition of the faculties of nature.
Fortitude and wisdom, justice and prudence are more precious to the mind than health is to the body.
Foolish is he who looks at another's fault, but does not consider his own defect. The obstinate person speaks without reflection. Every guilty person is incapable of advocating his own cause. The mind languishes and often labors under listlessness from the forgetfulness of the presence of God, "who searches the loins and hearts." The desire for better things, when it possesses the things desired and enjoys them, compels one to despise and avoid lesser things.
The work of obstinacy is contradiction. Every evil person stirs up contradictions, but the Lord shall send a merciless angel against him.
Intemperance is that which has no mastery over the passions that disturb it. Bodily afflictions and external distresses are related to the prevention of sin: "I have afflicted you," he says, "and oppressed you with hunger, that I might do you good before your iniquity is poured out to an immeasurable degree."
The evil wills of women submerge the weak minds of their husbands. It is characteristic of an imbecile mind, and one possessing no strength from hope in God, to be greatly cast down and shaken by afflictions. Sin is a disease of the soul; it is the death of the immortal soul. Many who are possessed by ancient habit do not discern the foulness of their actions.
It is impossible for some disturbance not to arise in the human mind from temptations. Since, therefore, we commit small and light sins, just as plants are moved by a gentle breeze, we are stirred in a quiet way; but when sins are more numerous and greater, in accordance with the growth of the sins, the agitation is naturally destined to intensify.
The unsociable and avaricious man is delivered to the fire before all other sinners. It is the character of a holy man to pass through this life, but to hasten toward the other. Therefore David says: "I am a sojourner before you, and a stranger, as were all my fathers before God."
Luxurious wine is a mocker, and drunkenness is boisterous. Bodily enjoyments have more of pain than of pleasure: marriages have sterility, widowhood, and infidelities; agriculture has infertility; merchandise has shipwrecks; riches have snares; delights, drunkenness, and continuous pleasures have multiple diseases and multiform sorrows.
Vigorous health to the extreme is perilous; it is subject to reversals and transitions, as nature falters, unable to sustain the burden of such well-being.
The beginning of the impulse toward virtue is the cessation of evil.
Gregory the Theologian. — The commandments of God look to nothing else but the salvation of one who obeys them.
Virtue is a masterless thing and voluntary; for what is constrained and imposed by force cannot be virtue.
Without prudence, none of one’s duties can be well performed.
It is a thing worthy of love, which needs no association with another, but bears within itself the finest qualities.
It is better to trust in veins and letters written on water than in the favor of men. For what reason? I believe it is so that, seeing the instability and inconstancy in these things, we may rather run back to God and to the future life, and take some thought for ourselves, considering the brevity of shadows and dreams.
Whatever a man persuades himself is to be feared, that he suffers, even if he does not suffer it; and he is tortured more by his own self than by those who inflict it.
Some of the friends of God live happily, while others, no whit worse, suffer bitter things: who knows the reasons for this, save Thee, O Word?
A better hope has separated male and female; but nature has a hidden disease within.
 It is better that God administers our affairs than that we should foresee them ourselves.
Would that wickedness might perish, and its first origin, and the evil one who sows the tares while we sleep, so that the beginning of evil becomes the neglect of the good.
For it touches not only the many, but also the best of men.
For simple-mindedness is the only thing that is entirely unassailable and invulnerable to the passions, so that it alone belongs to God.
Contumely does not touch him who has trained himself through humility.
Store up for the age that is beyond the end; for this present age fails even before the end.
Between two friends, truth is the more friendly.
[S] Chrysostom. — Calamities stir even the wise man to utter some word unworthy of his character.
Those diabolical harlots know the wise through the habit of their manner of life, and avoid them.
It is impossible for one who has accustomed his mouth to swear not to commit perjury frequently.
Every word supported by the aid of deeds is strong and firm; but every voice stripped of the testimony of actions is a sound that is fleeting, cast from the lips into the air.
Honors benefit the worthy, but they cast those who enjoy them without merit into greater punishment.
John Climacus. — A vigilant eye cleanses the mind; but an abundance of sleep makes the soul callous.
An ungrateful and deceitful friend is flesh, and when it is pampered, it does more harm.
man haughty of heart desires to rule; otherwise, as happens, he cannot perish at the end, or rather, he does not even wish to.
Simplicity is a habit of the soul that becomes invariable against perversity.
One cannot see simplicity separated from humility.
Love and humility are a holy union; for the former exalts, and the latter never ceases to restrain those who have been exalted.
The air, when cleansed of clouds, reveals the bright sun, and the soul, when deemed worthy of the remission of preconceptions, has seen the divine light in every way.
It is shameful for one who has heard a useful word not to learn it, and not to receive some good thing that is given by those who are kind.
Of Gregory the Theologian. — A timely rest is necessary for the health of the body.
To do good to an ungrateful person is to be reckoned the same as anointing a corpse.
Of Plato. — Without prudence it is not permitted to live well and justly; for where this is missing, life is not worth living.
Verses of George of Pisidia:
For always, as is fitting, calamities,
Irritated by fresh troubles,
Bring forth an impulse stronger than any counsel,
Especially when, according to their nature,
They add stimulus to a more fierce audacity.
Those who depart this life have said farewell
To sorrows, cares, and tribulations.
The uncoordinated is the corruptor of the heart,
The awakener of darkness, that first serpent,
Which from without introduces pleasures within
And sows base thoughts in the heart for the generation of sin.
The chorus of angels exults
Because of man turned from wickedness.
No man is without faults.
Of Babrius. — He who proffers lies against another becomes the craftsman of evils.
You will accomplish something more by persuasion than by acting with force.
To live without danger in a modest state is better than in a splendid one.
 Circumstances often happen to bring to light things worthy of silence, even contrary to expectation.
Favors which hasten to be given at the first opportune moment are found to be great.
It is not only present evils that are accustomed to afflict and grieve men, but already the expectation of future ones as well.
Of Aristotle. — Being a man myself, I judge it foolish to reproach a person for their fortune; for if
one supposes that he is excellent and has virtue, he does not know if it will remain with him until evening; how, then, should one speak of this or reproach others?
Of Aesop:
The jaws of an old man are a staff.
Of Solomon, about not trusting inopportunely:
A double-tongued man reveals secrets in a council; but a faithful one covers matters with his breath.
It is better to go to the house of mourning than to the house of feasting.
An injurious man stirs up quarrels; but he who guards his mouth and his tongue will keep his soul from distress.
man in impiety easily slanders.
thief is better than one accustomed to lying.
When the impious fall, the just shall be fearful.
man in labors works for himself and drives away his own destruction. The deceitful man, however, bears destruction in his own mouth.
An unwise man digs up evils for himself, and treasures up fire on his lips.
versatile tongue will fall into evils, but the pain of the unwise man is set over it.
An unrighteous man provokes friends, and leads them into paths that are not good.
An unwise man proclaims his anger immediately, but the crafty man treasures it up.
humble answer turns away wrath.
He who is silent pacifies contradiction; he who loves his life spares his mouth.
If you see a man quick in speech, know that the unwise man has more hope than he.
The dying just man ceases to repent.
crafty man is void of understanding.
Unpleasant things, when eaten with pain, are most sweet; for that which is prepared without sweat and provided readily is wont to be despised.
Of Menander:
Let the warrior be gentle in speech.
To speak beyond measure is the boldness of the tongue.
An evil man practices gentle speech; for he has his words like snares.
man of crooked ways has no friendships, for he loves to live in deceit and bites the soul. Flee the conversation of a crafty man; for he pours out poison and devours hearts. A prudent man does not have many words; for it is uncouth to speak superfluously. It is shameful for a man to depend in any part on one who is worse.
Having done shameful deeds, be castigated; but if you have done good, rejoice. It is the way of wise men, unless they are unjustly punished, to keep quiet; but for the good, if they suffer injustice, it is their way to leave peace for the sake of waging war, and if things go well, to enter into a treaty again after the war; and neither to be puffed up by their success in war, nor to tolerate injustices while enjoying the quiet of peace. For he who is sluggish out of desire for pleasure is quickly deprived of the very sweetness of leisure for which he is sluggish, if he has remained quiet; and he who is puffed up by success in war does not perceive that he is inflated by an untrustworthy audacity.
Which things, since they are grave for you, are in every way so for us also; for we make all things common with our friends, whether they go well or otherwise; which is also the law of friendship.
13. Confidently say that he is engaged in philosophizing in leisure, caring only as much about those who are doing him injury as he would about those if they existed whom he does not know.
They who are bound to the world are in anguish much more than those who serve God; but these have no reward for their anguish, whereas our pain will have a reward if we endure it for God’s sake.
Leisure without action is more noble than glory in vain deeds.
As soon as power has gained strength, it considers nothing more fitting than its own utility.
The beginning of practicing good things consists in the flight from evil things, for "Decline from evil," says the Psalmist, "and do good."
The splendors of the world manifest in themselves the lives of those who are observed.
Bodily pleasures have more agony than pleasure.
Of Saint Basil. — Violent discipline is not wont to last; but those things which insinuate themselves with pleasantness and grace remain in the soul for a longer time.
Life is but one day to those who labor with love.
14 Of those who are willing, not of those who are coerced, such is the mystery of salvation.
It is better to be corrected and cleansed here, than to be sent off to punishment there, where it is a time of torment, not of purification.
An earthly habitation weighs down a mind that is full of cares.
[A life] is brought quickly into suspicion of evil toward which it is hard to move toward wickedness.
It is better to have freedom from danger in speech that is not according to pleasure.
No one has ever been illustrious without life; although many have sounded well who were speakers; for the grace is not in those who speak, but in those who live well. B 
It is better to hear a word of exhortation than to recount impossibilities.
Proverbs. — A stone is heavy, and sand is a burden; but the folly of a foolish man is heavier than both.
A dart fixed in the thigh of the flesh, thus is a word in the stomach of a fool.
Good counsel will keep you, and holy thought will preserve you, that it may rescue you from an evil way and from a man who speaks no truth.
Do not seek things that are deeper than you, and do not examine things that are stronger than you. Think on those things that have been commanded to you; for you have no need of hidden things. C 
John. — Run through a mutable life with caution; for it provides many pitfalls, as you know.
Do not be sluggish toward doing good; for sluggishness has repentance.
Life is a pit and a snare to many.
A man finishing an obscure life cannot be well occupied with many cares.
Of Basil. — Any life that is with the body is more burdensome than every torment and every prison for him who hastens toward the life above.
Barbarian friendship, and barbarian kindness.
Food and drink are supports for human weakness.
The sumptuousness of foods delights the throat, but it feeds a worm of intemperance that never sleeps. D 
Of Chrysostom. — Envy of worthlessness obscures good things.
The words of the lascivious are soft.
The sluggard is compared to a stone covered with filth, and all will hiss at his disgrace.
The throat tastes food, but the mind judges words.
He who receives gifts in his bosom unjustly does not walk well in the ways.
Of the Theologian.
— The speech of the wise man is received into a teachable ear.
Of Babrius.
— Nature institutes every good thing, confirmed by word and by time.
It is not possible for him who does evil to remain hidden; for justice judges all things.
Of Aphthonius.
— They are excessively simple who wish to hide things that are evident.
Of Gennadius:
well-thinking mind, inclining to the right,
Despises all the pleasant things of life,
Riches, luxury, honors, and tumultuous words.
One often tames a lion with endearments;
Yet by giving it food, one stirs the belly to greater rage.
Of Chrysostom.
— It is necessary to be distressed for no other reason than for sin.
It happens that a lion is fierce by nature, but gentle contrary to nature; whereas a man is gentle by nature, but like the beasts contrary to nature.
If you desire to overcome mourning, walk around the tombs, and you will have a remedy for your pain, and you will see that among the greatest of human happinesses, they possess nothing beyond dust.
Of Gregory.
— Stones are true and firm words for those who have become savage.
One is truly not easily destroyed by hunger;
But for the wicked, death is a misery.
Only the needy seek the rich with insults,
Having no strength, but only words,
And words were repelled by words; but they were only words.
He who laughs at a physical defect is odious;
For it is a fault not of the will, but of fortune.
The shore is a harbor for a ship, and thought for mourning.
Sumptuous fare does not diminish great mourning.
Of Ecclesiastes.
— The words of the mouth of a wise man are grace, and the lips of the foolish cast him down.
sweet throat multiplies its friends, and a fluent tongue multiplies affabilities. A 
The redemption of a man’s soul is his riches.
Of the Apostle. — Be not overcome by evil, but overcome evil with good.
Of Solomon. — Do not give sleep to thine eyes, nor slumber to thine eyelids, that thou mayest be saved as a doe from the snares, and as a bird from the nets.
Do not love to speak much, lest thou be caught; for thy own lips are a fateful thing to a man. But he who is graceful in his lips shall be ensnared in his own entanglements.
Do not, when thou art poor, mimic a rich man; for the rich man has acted unjustly and raged, but the poor man has suffered injustice and shall be oppressed by threats as well. B 
A fool in laughter raises his voice; but a wise man will barely smile silently.
Do not devise evils against thy friend, who dwells with thee and trusts in thee.
Answer not a fool according to his folly, lest thou also become like unto him; but answer a fool according to his folly, lest he appear wise in his own eyes.
Do not be hasty in thy mind to be angry, for anger rests in the bosom of fools.
Do not be called a whisperer, and do not lie in wait with thy tongue; for the whisperer defiles his own soul.
Do not cease to do good to the poor, when thy hand has the power to help.
Of the Theologian. — Neither in joys cast away fear, nor in sorrows good hope. Remember both in the serenity of the tempest and in the storm the pilot; but do not fail in tribulations, nor become a bad servant, giving thanks to the Lord when He does good, but not following Him when He corrects. C 
The greatest support for the unfortunate is the hope of change; and better is that which lies before one’s eyes.
Be not, when you are poor, like the rich, nor affect to be wiser than the wise.
The only life is to look to the life above, the only death is sin; for the perdition of the soul is other things, which some who are proud imagine, playing with the phantom of a dream, and with the deceitful apparitions of the soul.
Babrius. — Do not believe everyone quickly, and especially not those who are not yet your friends.
Whips and chastisement are wisdom at every time.
To those who sin, forgiveness is left, namely to keep silent and to speak nothing about the things that have happened.
Do not be confident, being good, lest you fall; do not despair, being evil, that you may rise again.
John Climacus. — Great is he who remains insensate toward touch, but greater is he who remains invulnerable toward sight, and who conquers the sight of fire by the thought of the beauty above.
Do not believe the clay in your life, and do not be confident in it, until you meet Christ.
Do not be confident in abstinence, lest you fall: for he who eats nothing is cast down from heaven.
It is proper for a magnanimous and noble mind never to despair in any calamity; for the life of man on earth is a training ground.
Always preserve the remembrance of death, and despise all glory and wealth as fleeting rubbish.
Let the remembrance of death sleep with you, and the prayer of Jesus alone.
Basil. — Do not give leisure to your thoughts to examine another’s infirmity, but pay attention to yourself.
Do not let the pleasant joys of human things bring immoderate [agitation] into your soul; nor let mournful sadness and anguish diminish the strength and loftiness of the soul; for both are fleeting.
Drunkenness is the destruction of reason, the dissolution of strength, premature old age, and an early death.
Philo. — Do not let yourself be terrified by the inflations of the mind: for human things are but the shadows of shadows.
Blessed is the nature which rejoices in everything, and is pained by nothing, but gives thanks for those things which happen, as for things that are occurring well and beneficially.
Isocrates. — Do not love immoderate laughter,
nor approve a speech delivered with audacity; the one is the mark of a fool, the other of a madman. Never, when doing something shameful, hope to remain hidden; for even if you hide it from others, you are conscious of it yourself. Speak to no one, unless it is equally useful for the actions to be kept silent for you who speak, and for those who are listening. Do not make someone a friend, before you have examined how he has dealt with his former friends; for expect that he will be the same towards you as he has shown himself to be towards them. Do not try your friends by causing them loss, and do not lack the experience of acquaintances, but you will do this if, not being in need, you pretend to be in need. Upbraid no one for adversity; for fortune is common, and the future is unknown. Do not stand by or advocate for any wicked act; for you will appear to be doing such things yourself, if you bring aid to those who are acting so. Marci. — Do not say that virtue is obtained without difficulty; for that which comes from tranquility is illegitimate. Those who are wise in words think that only those who do wicked things are sinning; but those who are wise in spirit, even when suffering injustice, accuse themselves if they are not voluntarily enduring the injustices. Evagrii. — The greatest possession of blessings is virtue. Sexti. — Only he who possesses virtue is able to proclaim it according to its worth; for he praises that which he knows, and admires that which he has come to know. Consider it a great lesson to learn when you are speaking with one who is ignorant. Small favors, when timely, are great to those who receive them in adversity. David. — Greater is the danger of the judge than of the judged. Joannis. — The work of malice is perversity; for it always rejoices with those who are in adversity. Do not grieve, if you are adorned with the good things of the soul, if you are oppressed by the ailments of the body; rather, consider that this either prepares for you crowns, or suppresses your pride.
It belongs to God alone to be infallible and impassive.
I hate the glutton as the ruin of the soul.
The Theologian. — Do not be overly exalted, lest you fall more steeply.
Theoph... — Observed law removes injustices, but neglected, it brings down punishments.
A moderate sleep is the physician of illnesses.
Of Isocrates. — Consider nothing in human affairs to be constant; for thus you will neither be too jubilant in prosperity, nor too sorrowful in adversity.
The Theologian. — Reason is that which well directs both marriage and virginity, and as if it were matter, by this instrumental art, it is directed toward virtue.
To think disgraceful things and to speak them is equally unseemly. B Be sober, not drunken. For sin follows drunkenness, but upon sobriety follows the virtue of the soul.
An anxious mind is a worm gnawing at the bones;
But the body flourishes as it flees from cares.
The mass of the belly drives out the subtle mind;
For opposites battle with opposites.
An irascible mind is a teacher of wickedness.
Sloth is the wife of sleep.
We are stripped of those things which are not ours after death. For this (virtue or the mind) is eternal and immortal; but mortal things are ours for a brief time.
The fruits of the earth belong to specific times, but the fruits of friendship are born at all times.
Of Chrysostom. — Where there is an abundance of evils, there is a scarcity of goods.
This world itself is mortal and a resting place for those who are dying.
Whoever has need of something, even if he ought to seek what is just, awaits the opportunity of time, so that he may submit his petition to one who is gentle and well-disposed, and who is about to give.
When the mind becomes unfeeling, it yields to no one, neither to exhortation, nor to counsel, nor to admonition; but, darkened by passion, it is carried into the abyss.
He who desires future things, nor suffers from present evils...
is broken, nor is he puffed up by good things: but all things pass away, even as a traveler journeys.
Neither is the just man without sin, nor is the sinner entirely devoid of good.
Isocrates. These men cultivate friends only when present, but others love them even when far away. And a short time dissolves the associations of the wicked, but not even eternity can destroy the friendships of the virtuous.
Be affable, not harsh, not morose, not prone to strife, not quickly answering the anger of your companions; but appease those who are angry, and reprove those who are ceasing from their anger.
If you wish to make people your friends, say something good about them to those who will report it back to them; for the beginning of friendship is praise, but of enmity, reproach.
When you are about to ask advice of someone concerning your own affairs, look first at how he manages his own; for he who judges badly of his own affairs will never give good advice about those of others. But you will be especially spurred to seek advice if you look upon the calamities that have arisen from a lack of it. For we take the greatest care of our health when we remember the pains that have arisen from sickness.
Whatever you are about to say, examine it first in your mind; for with many, the tongue runs ahead of their thought.
C 65 Basil. As is the one who supervises and rules, such, for the most part, ought the subject to be.
Diseases are not entirely from nature, nor do they arise in us from an undisciplined diet or from certain other bodily causes, regarding which we sometimes see medicine being useful; for often diseases are the scourges of sins, brought on for our conversion.
There is no joy for prisoners in a dungeon,
Nor, in turn, for monks in worldly feasting.
D 66 The wicked man often assumes a good word or act as a mask; but it is impossible for the good man to assume any evil.
Neither do prosperous and happy things have anything in them that is lasting for the many; nor do calamitous and sinister things remain firmly fixed; but all things are subject to a certain agitation, and wavering, and unexpected changes.
Basil. — He who is not in need can endure necessities, but he who abounds in wealth can endure evils.
As riches increase, so much the more do they bring worry into life.
Judge your brother even as you yourself are judged by an equal measure.
The time of our life is such: the past has vanished, and the present, before it is tasted, already escapes perception. The nature of things is such: even as it grows or wanes, it is seen to have no persistence or constancy.
A shelter for a roof, and old age requires the avoidance of pain.
Anger is accustomed to disturb the soul more than other passions: for they say that anger rests in the bosom of fools.
Theognis.
I do not desire to become rich, nor do I pray for it; but may it be permitted to me to live with little, having no evil.
Diadochus. — Dreams are either images of wandering thoughts, or the illusions of demons.
Babrius. — God does not heed prayers that are offered to Him by iniquitous and impious mouths, while they have not yet ceased from evil.
Those who attempt to inflict harm upon others miserably injure themselves.
Theologus. — Intent is insufficient, unless we proceed through actions; the work reveals the intention.
Whatever our occupations may be in this life, such necessarily will our dreams be as well.
He who is not worthy of punishment is already worthy of honor; just as he who is not worthy of honor is therefore worthy of punishment.
What food is in the body, that is reason in the mind.
Not every peace is to be loved (for I know that, just as there is a most excellent discord, there can also be a most harmful concord), but that which is good and reconciles one to the good God.
Nothing is so precious to a righteous man as purity or purification.
Those who imitate the threads of spiders;
And who delight in the fragile things of life,
Let them know they are easily carried off by the winds,
All these delights of the life that imitates the spider.
One must not fear anger against those who are fiercely angry;
For the judge is one who judges those who are like himself.
Wine does not know how to compose nature,
But rather stirs it up, inciting it with pleasures.
To fall is not the lot of those who lie prostrate, but of those who are striving again. Walk with straight feet, O mortal, traversing an obscure life.
Justini. — Let the good man have a good man as a friend, but the bad man neither a good one nor a bad one.
Joannis. — That man is not pure who guards clay, but he who subjects its members perfectly to the mind.
He who wishes to overcome the demon of gluttony and the weariness of fornication is like one who extinguishes a fire with oil.
haughty eye will be humbled.
What the mind does not possess, the mouth does not bring forth.
It is impossible to find a life void of all pain.
Gennadii.
Drinking wine in moderation, not to the point of drunkenness,
You will obtain strength that is secure and legitimate.
Replenished with wine, and eating immoderately,
You will not command your deadly pleasures.
That which someone has not experienced, let him not...
give his assent.
It is not safe to swim with robes on, nor to apply that which has passions to theology.
There is nothing more senseless than the man the earth produces.
Cyril. — God is bountiful in beneficence; but He awaits the legitimate will of each person.
Sextus. — Not he who is rich is blessed, but he who is not in need of riches.
The shortness of life is not something to be mourned, but rather the fact that, being brief, it holds various cares.
Nothing so leads to love as not to judge anyone.
Solomon. — Let your eyes see right things, for they are before the eyes of God.
Wrath destroys even the wise.
He who conceals an offense seeks friendship; but he who refuses to conceal it separates friends and intimates.
Sextus. — Nothing among present things is pleasant, unless it is something that always remains.
Neither does a banquet without concord, nor riches without virtue help.
Solomon. — He who reveals a mystery has lost his faith.
He who believes with the confidence of his heart is a fool; but the wise conceal their opinion.
He who guards his mouth saves his soul; but he who is prompt with his lips shall terrify himself.
He who is pleasant in wine-bibbing in his fortresses leaves behind disgrace. C 
He who answers a word before he hears, it is foolishness and confusion for him.
There shall be no lover of drinking, if he has before his eyes the indecencies of the drunkards.
He who is prompt in speech shall be hated.
A slip from the pavement is better than a slip from the tongue.
 D He who leans on lies, he pastures the winds.
He who brings forth something excellent from one who is unworthy, shall be as my mouth.
Go not after your desires, and turn away from your counsels; and do not take pleasure in many delicacies, and do not be poured out on many eating occasions, for in many foods there shall be disease; and he who...
attends adds life to his days.
Treasures will not profit the ungodly, but righteousness delivers from death.
 An impatient man acts with imprudence, but a wise man endures much.
He who pities the poor is blessed.
The paths of life are the thoughts of the prudent, that, avoiding Hades, he might be saved.
The paths of those who avenge injuries lead to death.
The paths of the slothful are strewn with thorns.
Proverbs. — He who is of a perverse heart shall not find good.
An oblique man carries ruin in his own mouth.
 He who lacks intellect has no need of wisdom; for he is led rather by his own prudence.
The ways of the foolish are straight before them.
 [C 73] There is not so much need of guardians for orphan children as there is for senseless men.
He who desires more is deprived even of what he has.
Those who desire the greatest things do not attain even to the mediocre.
He who associates with the wicked will find the same destruction as they.
Wicked men exchange calamities for gifts of grace.
Hippocrates. — When the body is in its prime, then it is necessary to use the lightest diet.
Mark. — He who labors without consideration is always poor; but he who runs with hope is twice rich.
God is like an architect: what kind of materials we provide him, such are the houses he builds for us; if we provide him with beautiful and good deeds, from these he constructs for us beautiful dwellings.
God has cast us off and given us water of gall to drink, because we sinned against him. We gathered for peace, and there were no good things at the time of healing.
Habakkuk. — Because you have neglected me, says the Lord, your ways and your practices have done these things to you.
Woe to him who multiplies to himself what is not his! [D 74] He who accumulates evil avarice for his house.
Job. — My life is swifter than a runner.
Isocrates.
— Endeavor to be a lover of labor in body, but a lover of wisdom in mind, so that through the former you may be able to accomplish what you wish, and through the latter you may foresee what will be useful.
Pravitas is the root of all injustice.
Gird yourself to possess greater things when you are able; but strive to be considered a lover of equity, so that you may appear to study justice, not on account of weakness, but on account of moderation.
Concerning things about which you are ashamed to speak openly, and things which you wish to communicate to some of your friends, use words as if concerning another's affair; for thus you will discern their mind, and keep yourself hidden.
Act so that you may acquire riches, wealth, and possessions. Wealth belongs to those who know how to enjoy it, but possessions to those who know how to use them.
Charicles.
— Every passion, when known in time, finds a remedy, but when neglected, it is almost incurable.
To do shameful things and to speak them is equally unbecoming.
Young men are accustomed, if they are treated gently, to grow arrogant; if they are compelled by force, to yield.
See to it that you amend faults by persuasion and speech rather than by anger.
Chrysostom.
— Riches devoid of virtue do not make their possessor happy.
man is not rich because he possesses much, but because he gives much.
Many, accustomed from childhood to depraved habits and living licentiously, corrupt good natures; just as, conversely, some who are ill-born in body have, by a prudent life and suitable exercises, amended most of their vices.
Many among the rich consider life to be intolerable, and pray for a thousand deaths, when they experience some adversity, not knowing how to philosophize.
Marcus.
— The sign of old age is not gray hair, but the virtue of the soul.
Every business dealing is like a marketplace; he who knows how to trade gains much, but he who does not know suffers loss.
Dominicus, the hetaireiarches, Zoe dismisses Patriarch Nicholas together with his associates, in anger, telling them to attend to the concerns of their own church. 9. Not long after, Constantine the parakoimomenos slanders Dominicus the hetaireiarches to the Augusta, alleging that he was usurping the imperial power for his brother. Having made him a patrician under this pretext, when he came down to the church as was his custom to receive a blessing, they ordered him to remain at home. Zoe then put forward John Garidas as hetaireiarches and the eunuch Damianus as droungarios of the watch. Dominicus went off to his own house, lamenting in vain. 10. As Simeon of Bulgaria was again plundering Thrace, and the Augusta and those in authority were troubled about how to put an end to his arrogance, John the Bogas requested to be made patrician, promising to bring the Pechenegs against him. Having obtained his request and received gifts, he went off to the land of the Pechenegs; having made a treaty and taken hostages from there, he brought them to the City, the Pechenegs having agreed to cross over and wage war against Simeon. 11. There also arrived in the City Asotios, a man most famous for his strength, being the son of the Prince of Princes; it is said that he would take an iron bar, holding it by the ends with both hands, and with his overwhelming strength would bend it and twist it into a circular shape, the recalcitrant nature of the iron yielding to the force of his hands. Upon his arrival, they received him with great honor and sent him back again to his own land. 12. In the month of September, in the 3rd indiction, Pancratoukas the Armenian handed over Adrianople to Simeon. Not long after, Basil the patrician and kanikleios, and Nicetas, the Eladikian protospatharios, were sent by Zoe with many gifts, and they took it back again. 13. The emir Damianus also came to Strobilos with ships, intending to fight it with a great force; and he would have taken it, had he not fallen ill and died, the Hagarenes turning back in vain. 14. The inhabitants of Hellas and Athens, being constantly insulted by Chases, the son of Ioubes, unable to endure his debauchery, killed him miserably with stones inside the sanctuary of the temple in Athens. 15. The Augusta Zoe, seeing the exaltation of Simeon and his attack upon the Christians, consults with those in authority to arrange an exchange and peace with the Hagarenes, and to have the entire Eastern army cross over to fight and destroy Simeon. Therefore, John the patrician, the Radinos, and Michael the Toxaras were sent to Syria to effect the exchange. This having been done, they distributed the customary donative to the regiments, and taking them up along with the themes, they crossed over to the Thracian regions; Leo Magister Phokas being then domestikos of the schools, to whom endurance was attributed more than administrative prudence. When Constantine, the protopapas of the palace, called Kephalas, and Constantine Malelias brought out the venerable and life-giving woods in Thrace, all worshipped and swore to die with one another and marched forth with the whole army against the Bulgarians. John Grapson led the regiment of the Exkoubitores, and the son of Maroules led the Hikanatoi. Romanus Argyros was general, as were Leo his brother and Bardas Phokas; with them also were Melias and his Armenians, and all the other generals of the themes. Also with them for other matters was Constantine the patrician, the Lips, acting as counselor to Leo the domestikos in all things pertaining to the task. 16. On the 20th of the month of August, in the 5th indiction, the war between the Bulgarians and the Romans was joined at the river Achelous; and such were the judgments of God, as they are unsearchable and past finding out, the Romans turn in rout with their whole army, and there occurred a total flight and a frightful lamentation, some being trampled by one another, others being slaughtered by the enemy, and such a shedding of blood as had not happened since the beginning of time. Leo the Domestikos escaped by fleeing to Mesembria. There were slaughtered alongside...
Evagrius. — Words without sense are noise. A 
Wine strengthens the body, but the word of God strengthens the mind.
Sextus. — It is easy to exhort to things that are impossible to do.
It happens that an evil man may arrive at the sense of a good man. For the beneficence of the better sort is accustomed to urge those who are failing and slothful in spirit to obtain virtue.
Nothing is more useful than timely silence.
Not ruling yourself, do not presume to rule others.
Human affairs are a shadow, and not prudence.
Think of God more assiduously than you breathe.
A sign of ignorance is much speaking.
Slippery peaks turn the simple-minded toward insolence.
Idleness, without the fear of God, teaches wickedness to those who use time badly.
Philo. — Verbosity is the work of a sophist, but the work of a wise man is the exploration of natural things with modesty and benevolence.
Evagrius. — Use foods that are not pleasant, but salutary.
Climacus. — Friends are present in memory, even if they are absent in body.
He who is wise in heart will receive commandments, but he who is intemperate in his lips will be caught in his own snares.
The impious despise wisdom and instruction.
Wisdom is proclaimed in the ways, and speaks openly in the streets.
Wise men will hide their judgment, but the mouth of the inconsiderate is near to destruction. C 
Proverbs. — The mouth of fools brings forth evils.
The mouth of the truthful will be filled with joy.
The mouth of the just will distill wisdom.
Rarely set foot in your friend's house, lest he be sated and hate you.
A 80 ... Turn away from yourself insulting words.
Wisdom of man illuminates his face; but an impudent one will be hated for his face.
The mouth of the thoughtless is near to destruction, but he who saves his lips is prudent.
A slippery mouth produces ruins, and a fool multiplies words.
It will be more agreeable to live with a lion and a dragon than to dwell with an irascible and quarrelsome woman.
An equal weight is acceptable to the Lord.

Of Chrysostom. — It is not easy for everyone to cure a sick body; but for those who are sick in mind, it is easy for all to be cured.
Experience is the salvation of every man.
The present life is not unlike a stage; for present things are games, which do not remain forever.
The affairs of life are more hollow than a shadow and a shadow, full of many dangers.
Of Basil.
Moderation of speech is the purity of the heart.
Arist. — The life of men appears to be like a cucumber; for its extremities are bitter on both sides. C 
Plato. — The life of men appears to be a mere point.
Of John.
Only humility saves man.
Of John the monk. — For the body, it is good to die quickly; for it is freed from many evils of life. For the soul, it is difficult to die, because the death of the soul is sin.
Sirach. — He who teaches a fool is like one who repairs earthenware.
An inept man clearly leads a dangerous life.
A wise man, when asked by someone, "What is stronger than a statue?" replied, "An insensitive man."
Of Gennadius.
As for an introduced person, when there is need, do not possess him as a friend;
For he has no intention toward what is good.
The heart of a wise man is a haven of thoughts.
Of Sextus. — Household goods which are new are better; but friendship which is old.
The mouth of the prudent shall be sought in the Church.
Do not have frequent conversations with the same people, nor long ones about the same things; for there is a satiety of all good things.
The body is accustomed to grow by moderate labors, the mind by useful words.
 Fear God, honor parents, revere friends, obey the laws, seek pleasures which exist with decorum. For pleasure conjoined with what is good is best; but without the good, it is worst.
Be affable in manners; affability is to be otherwise toward those one meets, and urbanity is to converse familiarly with them in speech.
Value the riches you possess for two reasons: that they can pay a large fine, and can provide aid to an honest and unfortunate friend.
Do good to good men; for a huge treasure is gratitude owed by an honest man.
One must beware of the depraved, lest by finding an opportunity they do harm.
A prince ought to have prudence for opportunities, boldness against adversaries, and kindness toward those who are subject to him.

The greatest of evils the poor escape, the snares.
envy and hatred, to which the rich are daily exposed.
Philonis. — What is done without deliberation is all base, just as what is done with deliberation is noble.

Climaci. — To exercise oneself in the prudence of speech is an effective remedy for lust.
Laughter, like salt, should be used sparingly.
Charicleae. — Beautiful even are barbarian hands, it seems, to be revered.
If someone is accustomed to lying, he is not believed even in those things which he happens to say truly.
Aphthonii. — Greater danger is always found in lower things.

Do not seek in vain what is of no benefit.
Chrysostomi. — Benefits bring the well-minded to obey commands more than punishments do.
Salomonis. — A merciful man does good to his own soul.
The eyes of the wise are in his head; but the fool walks in darkness.
Theologi.
Though noble by birth, yet ignoble in character,
I count him dead, even if he smells sweet.
Noble birth is having good character. 
It is equally difficult to acquire some good thing that is lacking, and to preserve what is acquired. 
The word influences some, examples guide others; the latter need a stimulus, the former a bridle; for those who are lazy and sluggish toward the good are to be awakened by the lash of the word. 
Exhortation corrects some, reproach others; and this, for those who are reproved in public, or those who are admonished in secret. 
For the faint-hearted, it is a great thing to flee punishment; for the magnanimous, to attain rewards. 
There are three orders of those who are saved: slaves, hirelings, sons. If you are a slave, dread stripes; if a hireling, see only to receiving your reward; if you are above these and are a son, revere the Father; perform the good work, because it is good to obey the Father.
The wheel is fixed unstably, 
This life of ours is short and manifold; 
It moves above and is dragged below. 
It does not stand, even if it seems to stick; 
Fleeing, it is held fast, and cannot flee; 
It strives to snatch movement from immobility, 
So that you would say life is nothing, 
Except smoke, dream, or the flower of the grass. 
Basil. The person who is conspicuous in wickedness attracts many of the easily led into a similar zeal. 
Unfeelingness is untroubled, but sympathy is sorrowful, and a persistent thought of the heart.
Humility is not he who despises himself (for how will he bear himself?), but he who, having been wronged by a friend, preserves his love undiminished. 
Evil is not a living and animate substance, but a disposition in the soul contrary to virtue, coming about in the slothful through their falling away from the good. 
What we have gained through labor, we receive with joy and preserve with diligence; but of those whose acquisition is easy, their possession is held in contempt. 
The graces of good works return to those who give them. 
Evil is without substance, because it does not subsist from that which exists.
